\bar{f}_{i}(\bm{r}+\bm{c}_{i}\delta t,t+\delta t)-\bar{f}_{i}(\bm{r},t)=-\frac{\chi}{\bar{\tau}}\left(\bar{f}_{i}(\bm{r},t)-\bar{f}_{i}^{\rm eq}(\bm{r},t)\right)+\delta t(1-\frac{\delta t}{2\bar{\tau}})\mathcal{F}_{i}\bar{f}_{i}^{\rm eq}(\bm{r},t),

(760)

with,

\mathcal{F}_{i}=\frac{(\bm{c}_{i}-\bm{u})\cdot(\bm{F}-\bm{\nabla}V)}{RT}\\
-b\rho\chi\left[(\bm{c}_{i}-\bm{u})\cdot\left(\bm{\nabla}\ln\rho^{2}\chi T+\frac{3}{5}\left(\frac{{(\bm{c}_{i}-\bm{u})}^{2}}{2RT}-\frac{5}{2}\right)\bm{\nabla}\ln T\right)+\frac{2}{5}\left(\frac{(\bm{c}_{i}-\bm{u})\otimes(\bm{c}_{i}-\bm{u})}{RT}:\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}+\left(\frac{{(\bm{c}_{i}-\bm{u})}^{2}}{2RT}-\frac{5}{2}\right)\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{u}\right)\right].

(761)

The discrete equilibrium distribution function was obtained as a second-order polynomial expansion. While in
[
149
]
the authors only reported the theory and derivation of the model, it was later reprised in
[
262
]
and used for 1-D and 2-D simulations using a MRT realization.

7.2
Multiple components

Another area of active research to extend the range of application of non-ideal LBMs is the introduction of multiple components. While most of the application-oriented litterature in that area relies on the multi-component realization of the Shan-Chen model
[
263
]
, a few different attempts at other models have also been reported. Here we will briefly discuss some of the multi-component models developed within that context.

7.2.1
Ternary free energy model of Wöhrwag et al.

In an attempt to model ternary systems Wöhrwag et al.
[
264
]
introduced a free energy functional allowing three distinct minima, corresponding to one gas and two liquid components. The thermodynamics of the system is governed by two order parameters, namely density,
\rho
, and a phase field,
\phi
. As for the van der Waals fluid the total free energy density consists of two parts, bulk
\mathcal{A}
and interfacial; The bulk free energy is defined as:

\mathcal{A}=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}(\mathcal{A}_{\rm EoS}(\rho)-\mathcal{A}_{0})+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{2}C_{l1}^{2}(1-C_{l1})^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{2}C_{l2}^{2}(1-C_{l2})^{2},

(762)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\rm EoS}(\rho)
can be derived from integrating
the equation of state,
P=\rho(\partial\mathcal{A}_{\rm EoS}/\partial\rho)-\mathcal{A}_{\rm EoS}
, with coexisting liquid-gas densities at
\rho_{l}
and
\rho_{g}
. The two last terms in Eq.
762
have the form of double well potentials with
C_{l1}
and
C_{l2}
the relative concentrations of the two liquid components. Each term has two minima at
C_{\#}=0
(component absent) and
C_{\#}=1
(present). The relative concentration of the gas phase is defined as
C_{g}=(\rho-\rho_{l})/(\rho_{g}-\rho_{l})
, which is
0

for
\rho=\rho_{l}
and
1
for
\rho=\rho_{g}
. The relative concentrations are related to the density and phase field through

C_{l1}=\frac{1}{2}\left[1+\phi/\chi-(\rho-\rho_{l})/(\rho_{g}-\rho_{l})\right]
and
C_{l2}=\frac{1}{2}\left[1-\phi/\chi-(\rho-\rho_{l})/(\rho_{g}-\rho_{l})\right]
, with
\chi
a constant scaling parameter for
\phi
. This form of the bulk free energy leads to three minima located at
(\rho_{g},0)
,
(\rho_{l},+\chi)
and
(\rho_{l},-\chi)
. The bulk free energy map is shown in Fig.
59
.

Figure 59:
Contour plot of the bulk free energy density of Eq.
762
as a function of two order parameters,
\rho
and
\phi
. This plot is reproduced from
[
264
]
.

For the interfacial free energy density, the authors used

\mathcal{A}_{\rm Inter}=\frac{\kappa_{1}}{2}(\bm{\nabla}\rho)^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}(\bm{\nabla}C_{l1})^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{3}}{2}(\bm{\nabla}C_{l2})^{2},

(763)

which ca be written as a function of the order parameters
\rho
and
\phi
,

\mathcal{A}_{\rm Inter}=\left[\frac{\kappa_{1}}{2}+\frac{\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3}}{8(\rho_{g}-\rho_{l})^{2}}\right](\bm{\nabla}\rho)^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3}}{8\chi^{2}}(\bm{\nabla}\phi)^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{3}-\kappa_{2}}{4\chi(\rho_{g}-\rho_{l})}(\bm{\nabla}\rho\cdot\bm{\nabla}\phi),

(764)

which as expected includes three contributions, i.e. density gradient, phase field gradient and mixed density-phase field gradient. This new free energy functional only affects the NS equations through the total pressure tensor:

\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{P}_{\rm tot}=\rho\bm{\nabla}\mu_{\rho}+\phi\bm{\nabla}\mu_{\phi},

(765)

with the chemical potentials defined as:

\mu_{k}=\frac{\delta}{\delta k}\int\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{A}_{\rm Inter}dV\lvert_{T,k^{\prime}},

(766)

with
k\in\{\rho,\phi\}
and
k^{\prime}\in\{\phi,\rho\}
. The corresponding system of balance equations was solved using two distribution functions; One for the density and momentum fields and one for the balance of the phase field, i.e. Cahn-Hiliard equation. Using this model the authors successfully modeled collision of drops of immiscible liquids with density ratios of the order of
10^{3}
. An example is shown in Fig.
60
.

Figure 60:
Collision between two immiscible droplets (water and diesel oil). (a) experiments from
[
265
]
. (b),(c) simulation results. This figure is reproduced from
[
264
]
.

7.2.2
Mixtures with multi-component van der Waals equation of state

In
[
266
]
, Ridl & Wagner proposed a multi-component extension to the van der Waals second-gradient fluid. Starting with the single-component free energy:

\mathcal{A}_{\rm vdW}=\rho RT\ln\left(\frac{\rho}{1-b\rho}\right)-a\rho^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2}{\lvert\bm{\nabla}\rho\lvert}^{2},

(767)

they proposed an N-component van der Waals free energy defined as
[
266
]
:

\mathcal{A}_{\rm vdW-MC}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\rho_{k}RT\ln\left(\frac{\rho_{k}}{1-\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N}b_{k^{\prime}}\rho_{k^{\prime}}}\right)-\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N}\left(a_{kk^{\prime}}\rho_{k}\rho_{k^{\prime}}-\frac{\kappa_{kk^{\prime}}}{2}\bm{\nabla}\rho_{k}\cdot\bm{\nabla}\rho_{k^{\prime}}\right),

(768)

with the total bulk thermodynamic pressure obtained as:

P=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\rho_{k}RT}{1-\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N}b_{k^{\prime}}\rho_{k^{\prime}}}-\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N}a_{kk^{\prime}}\rho_{k}\rho_{k^{\prime}}.

(769)

To model the dynamics of the corresponding system they proposed a set of
N
discrete distribution functions, one for each component, evolving as
[
266
,
267
]
:

f_{ik}(\bm{r}+\bm{c}_{i}\delta t,t+\delta t)-f_{ik}(\bm{r},t)=\frac{\delta t}{\bar{\tau}}\left(f_{ik}^{\rm eq}(\bm{r},t)-f_{ik}(\bm{r},t)\right)-\mathcal{F}_{ik},

(770)

where the BGK collision operator accounts for the considered component’s ideal contributions while the term
\mathcal{F}_{ik}
takes into consideration non-ideal effects and interaction with other components. The densities and velocities of each individual component are obtained as zeroth- and first-order moments of the corresponding distribution functions while the mixture density and velocity are obtained as the sum of the density of all components and the mass-averaged velocities, respectively. The term
\mathcal{F}_{ik}
contributes at first order,

\sum_{i}\bm{c}_{i}\mathcal{F}_{ik}=\bm{F}_{k},

(771)

where the force
\bm{F}_{k}
has two contributions: one from the gradient of chemical potential
\bm{F}_{\mu k}
and one friction between different components
\bm{F}_{fk}
modeling cross-diffusion effects. The chemical potential gradient contribution is readily obtained as difference between the single-component ideal pressure and multi-component non-ideal one while
\bm{F}_{fk}
is defined as

\bm{F}_{fk}=-\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N}\lambda_{kk^{\prime}}\frac{\rho_{k}\rho_{k^{\prime}}}{\rho_{k}+\rho_{k^{\prime}}}\left(\bm{u}_{k^{\prime}}-\bm{u}_{k}\right),

(772)

where
\lambda_{kk^{\prime}}
are related to binary diffusion coefficients. It is readily demonstrated that under the assumption of symmetry of the diffusion coefficients tensor, i.e.
\lambda_{kk^{\prime}}-\lambda_{k^{\prime}k}
the contribution of cross-diffusion to total momentum is zero:

\sum_{k=1}^{N}\bm{F}_{fk}=0.

(773)

The authors showed that in the hydrodynamic limit under the assumption of
\lambda_{kk^{\prime}}=\lambda
, i.e. all components have the same Lewis number, one recovers the N-component Cahn-Hiliard equations. It should also be noted that similar models for two-component non-ideal mixture were also proposed in
[
268
,
269
]
where the models were shown to recover non-ideal effects and the Fick diffusion, equivalent to the Maxwell-Stefan system for binary isothermal mixtures.

7.2.3
The multi-component pseudo-potential method

While initially developed for non-ideal fluids, extension of the model to multiple components were introduced in
[
263
]
. For a system with
N
components, the interaction force on component
k
is given by:

\bm{F}_{k}=-\psi_{k}(\bm{r})\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N}G_{kk^{\prime}}\psi_{k^{\prime}}(\bm{r}+\bm{c}_{i}\delta t)\bm{c}_{i},

(774)

where
G_{kk^{\prime}}
sets the interaction strength between components
k
and
k^{\prime}
and forms a symmetrical tensor, i.e.
G_{kk^{\prime}}=G_{k^{\prime}k}
. Interestingly enough, upon application of a Taylor expansion the force is:

\bm{F}_{k}=-\psi_{k}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{N}G_{kk^{\prime}}\bm{\nabla}\psi_{k^{\prime}}+\frac{G_{kk^{\prime}}}{3}\bm{\nabla}\Delta\psi_{k^{\prime}}+\mathcal{O}(\bm{\nabla}^{5}).

(775)

As for the single-component non-ideal model, it leads to fixed surface tension coefficients and terms that are function of the pseudo-potential
\psi_{k}
instead of density
\rho_{k}
. Introduction of independent capillary coefficients can be readily introduced following the approaches used for the single-component version of the model, i.e. multiple-range interactions.

8
Conclusion

Application of the LBM to non-ideal fluids, and multi-phase flows in general, has been the focus of intense work from the early days of that method with first publications appearing in the early 90s. This rapid extension and growth in popularity is, in part, due to the simple transition from ideal to non-ideal fluids with interfaces in the context of LBM, illustrated best by the simplicity of the original pseudo-potential model.

Contrary to diffuse-interface models like the Allen-Cahn equation, the non-ideal LBM models come with interfacial properties, e.g. surface tension, interface thickness, Tolman length etc, linked to bulk physical properties of the considered fluid and follow the van der Waals thermodynamics of interfaces. The interface properties are therefore, in principle, dictated by the thermodynamics of the considered fluid. This was illustrated via the example of a
{\rm N}_{2}
liquid/vapor interface showing a thickness of the order of
10^{-7}
m, see Fig.
20
, limiting the grid-size and preventing simulations at larger realistic scales.
As a result they can be perceived as models able to cover a wider range of parameters as compared to classical sharp and/or diffuse interface methods: In the limit of thick interfaces where its width is comparable to the flow characteristic size they recover the van der Waals fluid thermodynamics (or a modified form of it in the case of the pseudo-potential method) and the Korteweg stress tensor at the macroscopic level and can be derived from the Enskog-Vlasov approximation at the level of the kinetic theory. In the limit of thin interfaces, the extreme computational cost imposed by the
physical
thickness of the interface can be considerably reduced by relying on the principle of corresponding states.

Non-ideal LBM-based solvers are also subject to a number of issues such as stability resulting in limited range of accessible non-dimensional viscosities, thermodynamic inconsistency leading mismatch in coexistences densities at smaller temperatures, spurious currents appearing at interfaces as a result of the non-isotropy of the discrete system etc. A wide variety of solution to eliminate or reduced the effect of these issues have been proposed allowing simulation with larger density ratios, Weber and Reynolds number to be conducted. With the most recent development around these approaches they have become quite competitive and accurate tools for simulations in the lower Weber number regimes in areas such as microfluidics, drop interaction with solid surfaces etc. For much larger Weber numbers and applications such as multiphase jets with primary and secondary break-up further improvement is needed, especially to extend the range of accessible surface tensions to lower values at given interface thicknesses and acceptable levels of spurious currents. Given the kinetic roots of some of the models in that class of solvers, extension of the models to higher order physics beyond the meanfield approximation would also be a possible area of developmenet in the feature.

Declaration of interests

The authors report no conflict of interest.

Appendix A
Hermite expansion

Single variable Hermite polynomials.

The single variable Hermite polynomial
\mathcal{H}_{n}
of order
n
of a variable
x
is defined as:

\mathcal{H}_{n}(x)=\frac{{(-1)}^{n}}{w(x)}\frac{d^{n}}{dx^{n}}w(x),

(776)

where the normalized function
w(x)
is defined as:

w(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}.

(777)

Based on this definition, the first few polynomials can be computed as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}

\displaystyle=1,

(778a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1}

\displaystyle=x,

(778b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{2}

\displaystyle=x^{2}-1,

(778c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{3}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-3x,

(778d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{4}

\displaystyle=x^{4}-6x^{2}+3,

(778e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{5}

\displaystyle=x^{5}-10x^{3}+15x,

(778f)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{6}

\displaystyle=x^{6}-15x^{4}+45x^{2}-15.

(778g)

These polynomials are mutually orthogonal with respect to the weight function,
w(x)
, i.e.:

\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\mathcal{H}_{m}(x)w(x)\mathcal{H}_{n}(x)dx=n!\delta_{mn},

(779)

where
\delta_{mn}
is the Kronecker delta function. Furthermore, they form a complete orthogonal basis of the Hilbert space of functions
f(x)
satisfying:

\displaystyle\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}{\lvert f(x)\rvert}^{2}w(x)dx<\infty.

(780)

As such, one can express the function
f(x)
as:

f(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\bm{a}_{n}\mathcal{H}_{n}(x),

(781)

where
\bm{a}_{n}
is the order
n
Hermite coefficient. Multiplying both sides by
\mathcal{H}_{m}(x)w(x)
and integrating over
x
:

\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\mathcal{H}_{m}(x)w(x)f(x)dx=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\bm{a}_{n}\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\mathcal{H}_{m}(x)w(x)\mathcal{H}_{n}dx,

(782)

and using the mutual orthogonality of Hermite polynomials, we get an expression for the Hermite coefficients as:

\bm{a}_{m}=\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\mathcal{H}_{m}(x)w(x)f(x)dx.

(783)

Alternatively, one can also expand the function
f(x)
as:

f(x)=w(x)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\bm{a}_{n}\mathcal{H}_{n}(x),

(784)

resulting in the following expression for the coefficient
\bm{a}^{(m)}
:

\bm{a}_{m}=\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\mathcal{H}_{m}(x)f(x)dx.

(785)

To better illustrate this, let us consider the example of the following function:

f(x)=\rho\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\theta}}e^{-\frac{{(x-u)}^{2}}{2\theta}}.

(786)

This function can be shown to be square-integrable with respect to the previously-defined weight function. As such the corresponding Hermite coefficients can be computed through Eq.
785
:

\bm{a}_{m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\theta}}\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\mathcal{H}_{m}(x)e^{-\frac{{(x-u)}^{2}}{2\theta}}dx,

(787)

which using the change of variable
\eta=(x-u)/\sqrt{\theta}
can be re-written as:

\bm{a}_{m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\mathcal{H}_{m}(\sqrt{\theta}\eta+u)e^{-\frac{{\eta}^{2}}{2}}d\eta.

(788)

The different order coefficients can be easily evaluated using the following integral:

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x^{k}e^{-ax^{2}}dx=\begin{cases}0&k=2k^{{}^{\prime}}+1\\
\frac{(2k^{{}^{\prime}}-1)!}{{(2a)}^{k^{{}^{\prime}}}}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{a}}&k=2k^{{}^{\prime}}\end{cases},

(789)

leading to the following expansion:

f(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\rho w(x)}{n!}\bm{a}_{n}\mathcal{H}_{n}(x).

(790)

The first few terms are given in Table
8
.

Hermite polynomials and coefficients for the Gaussian distribution function

Multivariate Hermite polynomials.

In a
D
-dimensional space the Hermite polynomial of order
n
is defined as:

\bm{\mathcal{H}_{n}}\left(\bm{x}\right)=\frac{{(-1)}^{n}}{w\left(\bm{x}\right)}\bm{\nabla}^{n}w\left(\bm{x}\right),

(791)

where
\bm{\nabla}^{n}
is the
n^{\text{th}}
order derivative resulting in a tensor of rank
n
and
w\left(\bm{x}\right)
is the weight function defined as:

w\left(\bm{x}\right)=\frac{1}{{2\pi^{D/2}}}e^{-\frac{\bm{x}^{2}}{2}}.

(792)

Orthogonality of the multivariate Hermite polynomials results in:

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}w\left(\bm{x}\right)\bm{\mathcal{H}_{i}}\left(\bm{x}\right):\bm{\mathcal{H}_{j}}\left(\bm{x}\right)d\bm{x}=\begin{cases}0&i\neq j\\
n!\bm{\delta_{ij}}&i=j\end{cases},

(793)

where
\bm{i}
and
\bm{j}
are abbreviations for the set of indices {
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}
} and {
j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{n}
} respectively, and
\bm{\delta_{ij}}
is equal to unity if
\bm{i}
is a permutation of
\bm{j}
and zero otherwise. In a 3-D space the first few Hermite polynomials are computed as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}

\displaystyle=1,

(794a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{i}

\displaystyle=x_{i},

(794b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{ij}

\displaystyle=x_{i}x_{j}-\delta_{ij},

(794c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}-\left(\delta_{ij}x_{k}+\delta_{ik}x_{j}+\delta_{jk}x_{i}\right),

(794d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{ijkl}

\displaystyle=x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}-\left(\delta_{ij}x_{k}x_{l}+\delta_{ik}x_{j}x_{l}+\delta_{il}x_{j}x_{k}+\delta_{jk}x_{i}x_{l}+\delta_{jl}x_{i}x_{k}+\delta_{kl}x_{i}x_{j}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right),

(794e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{ijklm}

\displaystyle=x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}x_{m}-\left(\delta_{lm}x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}+\delta_{km}x_{i}x_{j}x_{l}+\delta_{kl}x_{i}x_{j}x_{m}+\delta_{jm}x_{i}x_{k}x_{l}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\delta_{jl}x_{i}x_{k}x_{m}+\delta_{kl}x_{i}x_{l}x_{m}+\delta_{im}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}+\delta_{il}x_{j}x_{k}x_{m}+\delta_{ik}x_{j}x_{l}x_{m}+\delta_{ij}x_{k}x_{l}x_{m}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{m}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right)+x_{l}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{km}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{jk}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{k}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{jl}\right)+x_{j}\left(\delta_{ik}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{il}\delta_{km}+\delta_{im}\delta_{kl}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{i}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{jl}\delta_{km}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{kl}\right),

(794f)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{ijklmn}

\displaystyle=x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}x_{m}x_{n}-\left(x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}\delta_{mn}+x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{m}\delta_{ln}+x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{n}\delta_{lm}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+x_{i}x_{j}x_{l}x_{m}\delta_{kn}+x_{i}x_{j}x_{l}x_{n}\delta_{km}+x_{i}x_{j}x_{m}x_{n}\delta_{lk}+x_{i}x_{k}x_{l}x_{m}\delta_{jn}\right.

\displaystyle\left.x_{i}x_{k}x_{l}x_{n}\delta_{jm}+x_{i}x_{k}x_{m}x_{n}\delta_{jl}+x_{i}x_{l}x_{m}x_{n}\delta_{jk}+x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}x_{m}\delta_{in}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}x_{n}\delta_{im}+x_{j}x_{l}x_{m}x_{n}\delta_{ik}+x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}x_{n}\delta_{im}+x_{j}x_{k}x_{m}x_{n}\delta_{il}+x_{k}x_{l}x_{m}x_{n}\delta_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{i}x_{j}\left(\delta_{kl}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{km}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{kn}\delta_{lm}\right)+x_{i}x_{k}\left(\delta_{jl}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{ml}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{i}x_{l}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{kn}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{mk}\right)+x_{i}x_{m}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jl}\delta_{kn}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{lk}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{i}x_{n}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{jl}\delta_{km}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{lk}\right)+x_{j}x_{k}\left(\delta_{in}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{il}\delta_{nm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{ln}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{j}x_{l}\left(\delta_{in}\delta_{km}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{nm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{kn}\right)+x_{j}x_{m}\left(\delta_{in}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{nl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{kn}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{j}x_{n}\left(\delta_{il}\delta_{km}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{kl}\right)+x_{k}x_{l}\left(\delta_{mn}\delta_{ij}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{nj}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{in}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{k}x_{m}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jn}+\delta_{in}\delta_{lj}\right)+x_{k}x_{n}\left(\delta_{ml}\delta_{ij}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{lj}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{il}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{l}x_{m}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kn}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jn}+\delta_{in}\delta_{kj}\right)+x_{l}x_{n}\left(\delta_{mk}\delta_{ij}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{kj}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{ik}\right)

\displaystyle+x_{n}x_{m}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{kj}\right)+\delta_{ij}\left(\delta_{kl}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{km}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{kn}\delta_{ml}\right)

\displaystyle+\delta_{ik}\left(\delta_{jl}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{ml}\right)+\delta_{il}\left(\delta_{kj}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{km}\delta_{jn}+\delta_{kn}\delta_{mj}\right)

\displaystyle+\delta_{im}\left(\delta_{jl}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jk}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{kl}\right)+\delta_{in}\left(\delta_{kj}\delta_{ml}+\delta_{km}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{kl}\delta_{mj}\right).

(794g)

As for the single variable case, for a square-integrable function
f\left(\bm{x}\right)
, it can be expressed as:

f\left(\bm{x}\right)=w\left(\bm{x}\right)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\bm{a}_{n}:\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\left(\bm{x}\right),

(795)

where the Hermite coefficients
\bm{a_{n}}
are defined as:

\bm{a}_{n}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}f\left(\bm{x}\right)\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\left(\bm{x}\right)d\bm{x},

(796)

resulting in the following coefficients for the multi-variate version of the distribution function of Eq.
786
:

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{0}

\displaystyle=\rho,

(797a)

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{i}

\displaystyle=\rho u_{i},

(797b)

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\rho u_{i}u_{j}+\rho\left(\theta-1\right)\delta_{ij},

(797c)

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{ijk}

\displaystyle=\rho u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}+\rho\left(\theta-1\right)\left(\delta_{ij}u_{k}+\delta_{ik}u_{j}+\delta_{jk}u_{i}\right),

(797d)

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{ijkl}

\displaystyle=\rho u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}u_{l}+\rho\left(\theta-1\right)\left(\delta_{ij}u_{k}u_{l}+\delta_{ik}u_{j}u_{l}+\delta_{il}u_{j}u_{k}+\delta_{jk}u_{i}u_{l}+\delta_{jl}u_{i}u_{k}+\delta_{kl}u_{i}u_{j}\right)

\displaystyle+\rho{\left(\theta-1\right)}^{2}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right),

(797e)

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{ijklm}

\displaystyle=\rho u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}u_{l}u_{m}+\rho\left(\theta-1\right)\left(\delta_{lm}u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}+\delta_{km}u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}+\delta_{kl}u_{i}u_{j}u_{m}+\delta_{jm}u_{i}u_{k}u_{l}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\delta_{jl}u_{i}u_{k}u_{m}+\delta_{kl}u_{i}u_{l}u_{m}+\delta_{im}u_{j}u_{k}u_{l}+\delta_{il}u_{j}u_{k}u_{m}+\delta_{ik}u_{j}u_{l}u_{m}+\delta_{ij}u_{k}u_{l}u_{m}\right)

\displaystyle+\rho{\left(\theta-1\right)}^{2}\left[u_{m}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right)+u_{l}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{km}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{jk}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{k}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{jl}\right)+u_{j}\left(\delta_{ik}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{il}\delta_{km}+\delta_{im}\delta_{kl}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{i}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{jl}\delta_{km}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{kl}\right)\right],

(797f)

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{ijklmn}

\displaystyle=\rho u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}u_{l}u_{m}u_{n}+\rho\left(\theta-1\right)\left(u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}u_{l}\delta_{mn}+u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}u_{m}\delta_{ln}+u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}u_{n}\delta_{lm}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}u_{m}\delta_{kn}+u_{i}u_{j}u_{l}u_{n}\delta_{km}+u_{i}u_{j}u_{m}u_{n}\delta_{lk}+u_{i}u_{k}u_{l}u_{m}\delta_{jn}\right.

\displaystyle\left.u_{i}u_{k}u_{l}u_{n}\delta_{jm}+u_{i}u_{k}u_{m}u_{n}\delta_{jl}+u_{i}u_{l}u_{m}u_{n}\delta_{jk}+u_{j}u_{k}u_{l}u_{m}\delta_{in}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{j}u_{k}u_{l}u_{n}\delta_{im}+u_{j}u_{l}u_{m}u_{n}\delta_{ik}+u_{j}u_{k}u_{l}u_{n}\delta_{im}+u_{j}u_{k}u_{m}u_{n}\delta_{il}+u_{k}u_{l}u_{m}u_{n}\delta_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle+\rho{\left(\theta-1\right)}^{2}\left[u_{i}u_{j}\left(\delta_{kl}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{km}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{kn}\delta_{lm}\right)+u_{i}u_{k}\left(\delta_{jl}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{ml}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{i}u_{l}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{kn}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{mk}\right)+u_{i}u_{m}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jl}\delta_{kn}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{lk}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{i}u_{n}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{jl}\delta_{km}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{lk}\right)+u_{j}u_{k}\left(\delta_{in}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{il}\delta_{nm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{ln}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{j}u_{l}\left(\delta_{in}\delta_{km}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{nm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{kn}\right)+u_{j}u_{m}\left(\delta_{in}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{nl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{kn}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{j}u_{n}\left(\delta_{il}\delta_{km}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{lm}+\delta_{im}\delta_{kl}\right)+u_{k}u_{l}\left(\delta_{mn}\delta_{ij}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{nj}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{in}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{k}u_{m}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jn}+\delta_{in}\delta_{lj}\right)+u_{k}u_{n}\left(\delta_{ml}\delta_{ij}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{lj}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{il}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{l}u_{m}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kn}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jn}+\delta_{in}\delta_{kj}\right)+u_{l}u_{n}\left(\delta_{mk}\delta_{ij}+\delta_{mi}\delta_{kj}+\delta_{mj}\delta_{ik}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u_{n}u_{m}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{kj}\right)\right]+\rho{\left(\theta-1\right)}^{3}\left[\delta_{ij}\left(\delta_{kl}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{km}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{kn}\delta_{ml}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\delta_{ik}\left(\delta_{jl}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{jm}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{ml}\right)+\delta_{il}\left(\delta_{kj}\delta_{mn}+\delta_{km}\delta_{jn}+\delta_{kn}\delta_{mj}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\delta_{im}\left(\delta_{jl}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jk}\delta_{ln}+\delta_{jn}\delta_{kl}\right)+\delta_{in}\left(\delta_{kj}\delta_{ml}+\delta_{km}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{kl}\delta_{mj}\right)\right].

(797g)

Appendix B
Elements of the von Neumann formalism

Starting with a given set of coupled continuous/discretized PDEs, bound by periodic boundary conditions, defined as:

\mathcal{L}\left(f_{i},\bm{r},t\right)=0,

(798)

where
\mathcal{L}
is the time evolution operator, the equations have to be linearized in order to use the VN method. To achieve this for the LB system of equations one can expand (first-order Taylor-McLaurin expansion) the distribution function around a reference state
f_{i}\left(\bar{\rho},\bar{u}\right)
:

f_{i}\approx\bar{f}_{i}+f^{{}^{\prime}}_{i},

(799)

\delta_{t}\Omega_{i}(f_{i})\approx\delta_{t}\Omega_{i}\rvert_{\bar{f}_{i}}+J_{ij}f^{{}^{\prime}}_{j},

(800)

where Einstein’s notation (summation) over
j
is used, and for the sake of clarity,
\bar{f}_{i}=f_{i}\left(\bar{\rho},\bar{u}\right)
. Obviously, relying on a first-order expansion around the distribution function this expansion is only valid in the linear regime (i.e. small perturbations around the reference state). In addition,
J_{ij}
is the Jacobian of the collision operator evaluated about
\bar{f}_{j}
, i.e,
J_{ij}=\partial_{f_{j}}\delta_{t}\Omega_{i}\rvert_{\bar{f}_{j}}.
Placing back these expressions into the discrete LB time-evolution equation:

f_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}\left(\bm{r}+\bm{c}_{i}\delta t,t+\delta t\right)-f_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}\left(\bm{r},t\right)=J_{ij}f_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}\left(\bm{r},t\right)\\
-\underbrace{\left(\bar{f}_{i}\left(\bm{r}+\bm{c}_{i}\delta t,t+\delta t\right)-\bar{f}_{i}\left(\bm{r},t\right)-\delta t\Omega_{i}\rvert_{\bar{f}_{i}}\right)}_{=0},

(801)

and taking out the last terms on the RHS one gets:

f_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}\left(\bm{r}+\bm{c}_{i}\delta t,t+\delta t\right)=\left(\delta_{ij}+J_{ij}\right)f_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}\left(\bm{r},t\right),

(802)

where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta function.
Using the SRT collision operator for instance, we can then re-write the linearized time-evolution equation as:

f_{i}^{{}^{\prime}}\left(\bm{r}+\bm{c}_{i}\delta t,t+\delta t\right)=\left[\left(1-\frac{\delta t}{\bar{\tau}}\right)\delta_{ij}+\frac{\delta t}{\bar{\tau}}J_{ij}^{\rm eq}\right]f_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}\left(\bm{r},t\right),

(803)

with
J_{ij}^{\rm eq}=\partial_{j}f^{\rm eq}_{\alpha}\rvert_{\bar{f}_{j}}
and
\bar{f}_{j}=f^{\rm eq}_{j}(\bar{\rho},\bar{\bm{u}})
. To compute the Jacobian matrix of the EDF, knowing that
\partial_{f_{j}}f_{k}=\delta_{jk}
, the following expressions can be used:

\partial_{f_{j}}\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{0}=\partial_{f_{j}}(\rho)=\sum_{k}\delta_{jk}=1,

(804)

\partial_{f_{j}}\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{1}=\partial_{f_{j}}(\rho\bm{u})=\sum_{k}\bm{c}_{k}\delta_{jk}=\bm{c}_{j}.

(805)

Once re-written as a function of the conserved Hermite coefficients, computing the Jacobians of higher-order components of the Hermite expansion is straightforward. Let us consider the second-order Hermite coefficient for example:

\partial_{f_{j}}\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{2}=\partial_{f_{j}}\frac{\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{1}\otimes\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{1}}{\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{0}}=-\frac{\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{1}\otimes\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{1}}{(\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{0})^{2}}\\
+\frac{\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{1}\otimes\bm{c}_{j}+{\left(\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{1}\otimes\bm{c}_{j}\right)}^{\dagger}}{\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{0}}.

(806)

Eventually, for the second-order EDF the Jacobian reads:

J_{ij}^{\rm eq}=w_{i}\Bigg(\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{0}+\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(\bm{c}_{i}):\partial_{f_{j}}\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{1}+\bm{\mathcal{H}}_{2}(\bm{c}_{i}):\frac{\partial_{f_{j}}\bm{a}^{\rm eq}_{2}}{2}\Bigg).

(807)

The last step of the VN analysis is to assume that perturbations
f^{\prime}_{i}
are monochromatic plane waves :

f^{\prime}_{i}=F_{i}\exp{[\sqrt{-1}(\bm{k}\cdot\bm{r}-\omega_{i}t)]},

where
F_{i}
is the wave amplitude,
\sqrt{-1}
is the imaginary unit,
||\bm{k}||=k
is the wave-number, and
\omega
is the complex time frequency of the wave.
k
is related to the wave-length of
f^{\prime}_{i}
, whereas
\Im(\omega)
and
\Re(\omega)
are related to its attenuation and propagation speed. By injecting these perturbations into Eq.
802
one obtains the following eigenvalue problem of size
Q
:

\bm{MF}=\exp{(-\sqrt{-1}\omega_{i})}\bm{F},

(808)

where
\bm{F}
is the eigenvector composed of all amplitudes. It is related to the eigenvalue
\exp{(-\sqrt{-1}\omega)}
.
\bm{M}
is the matrix associated to Eq.
802
. Here this matrix can be expressed as :

\bm{M}=\bm{E}\left[\bm{\delta}+\bm{J}\right],

(809)

with

E_{ij}=\exp[-i(\bm{c}_{i}\cdot\bm{k})]\delta_{ij}.

(810)

It is important to notice that the matrix
\bm{M}
and the eigenvalue problem
808
depend on the mean flow (
\bar{\rho},\bar{\bm{u}}
), the wave-number (
k_{x}
and
k_{y}
in 2-D) and the relaxation coefficient
\bar{\tau}
, or equivalently the kinematic viscosity
\nu
. This means that for each set of these parameters the eigenvalue problem needs to be solved to obtain the corresponding values of
\Re{(\omega)}
and
\Im{(\omega)}
. Doing so, the spectral properties (dispersion and dissipation) can be obtained for any given collision model.

Appendix C
Hydrodynamic limit of the Enskog–Vlasov–BGK kinetic model

Chapman–Enskog analysis

Expanding the distribution function as:

f=f^{(0)}+\epsilon f^{(1)}+\epsilon^{2}f^{(2)}+{O}(\epsilon^{3}),

(811)

introducing it back into (
576
) and separating terms with different orders in
\epsilon
, at order zero one recovers:

f^{(0)}=f^{\rm eq}.

(812)

This latter implies the solvability conditions,

\displaystyle\int f^{(k)}d\bm{v}=0,\ \forall k\neq 0,

(813)

\displaystyle\int\bm{v}f^{(k)}d\bm{v}=0,\ \forall k\neq 0.

(814)

At order
\epsilon
:

\partial_{t}^{(1)}f^{(0)}+\bm{v}\cdot\bm{\nabla}f^{(0)}=-\frac{1}{\tau}f^{(1)}-\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial f^{\rm eq}}{\partial\bm{u}}\cdot\bm{F}^{(1)},

(815)

which, upon integration in
\bm{v}
, leads to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{u}=0,

(816)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho\bm{u}+\bm{\nabla}\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot P_{0}\bm{I}+\bm{F}^{(1)}=0.

(817)

At order
\epsilon^{2}
:

\partial_{t}^{(2)}f^{(0)}+\partial_{t}^{(1)}f^{(1)}+\bm{v}\cdot\bm{\nabla}f^{(1)}=-\frac{1}{\tau}f^{(2)},

(818)

which leads to the following equation for mass conservation:

\partial_{t}^{(2)}\rho=0,

(819)

while for momentum:

\partial_{t}^{(2)}\rho\bm{u}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left[\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}f^{(1)}d\bm{v}\right]=0.

(820)

The last term on the left hand side can be evaluated using the previous order in
\epsilon
as:

\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}f^{(1)}d\bm{v}=-\tau\left[\partial_{t}^{(1)}\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}f^{(0)}d\bm{v}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}f^{(0)}d\bm{v}\right.\\
\left.+\frac{1}{\rho}\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}\frac{\partial f^{\rm eq}}{\partial\bm{u}}\cdot\bm{F}^{(1)}d\bm{v}\right],

(821)

where:

\partial_{t}^{(1)}\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}f^{(0)}d\bm{v}=-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}-\left[\bm{u}\otimes(\bm{F}^{(1)}+\bm{\nabla}P_{0})+(\bm{F}^{(1)}+\bm{\nabla}P_{0})\otimes\bm{u}\right]+\partial_{t}^{(1)}P_{0}\bm{I},

(822)

and:

\bm{\nabla}\cdot\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}f^{(1)}d\bm{v}=\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}+\left[\bm{\nabla}P_{0}\bm{u}+{\bm{\nabla}P_{0}\bm{u}}^{\dagger}\right]+\bm{I}\bm{\nabla}\cdot P_{0}\bm{u},

(823)

\frac{1}{\rho}\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}\frac{\partial f^{\rm eq}}{\partial\bm{u}}\cdot\bm{F}^{(1)}d\bm{v}=\bm{u}\otimes\bm{F}^{(1)}+{\bm{F}^{(1)}\otimes\bm{u}},

(824)

which leads to:

\int\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}f^{(1)}d\bm{v}=-\tau\left[P_{0}\left(\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}+{\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}}^{\dagger}\right)+\left(\partial_{t}^{(1)}P_{0}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot P_{0}\bm{u}\right)\bm{I}\right],

(825)

where the last two terms can be re-written as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{(1)}P_{0}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot P_{0}\bm{u}=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial P_{0}}{\partial\rho}(\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{u})+\left(P_{0}-\rho\frac{\partial P_{0}}{\partial\rho}\right)\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{0}\left(1-\frac{\partial\ln P_{0}}{\partial\ln\rho}\right)\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{u},

(826)

in turn recovering the Navier-Stokes-level momentum equation:

\partial_{t}^{(2)}\rho\bm{u}-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\mu\left(\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}+{\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}}^{\dagger}-\frac{2}{3}\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{u}\bm{I}\right)-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\eta\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{u}\bm{I}\right)=0,

(827)

where:

\displaystyle\mu=\tau P_{0},

(828)

\displaystyle\eta=\tau P_{0}\left(\frac{5}{3}-\frac{\partial\ln P_{0}}{\partial\ln\rho}\right).

(829)

Appendix D
Chapman–Enskog analysis of the lattice Boltzmann method for non-ideal fluids

Using a Taylor expansion around
(\bm{r},t)
,

f_{i}\left(\bm{r}+\bm{c}_{i}\delta t,t+\delta t\right)-f_{i}\left(\bm{r},t\right)=\left[\delta t\mathcal{D}_{t}+\frac{\delta t^{2}}{2}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{t}\right]f\left(\bm{r},t\right)+{O}(\delta t^{3})

(830)

the discrete time-evolution equation is re-written as:

\delta t\mathcal{D}_{t}f_{i}+\frac{{\delta t}^{2}}{2}{\mathcal{D}_{t}}^{2}f_{i}+{O}({\delta t}^{3})=\frac{\delta t}{\bar{\tau}}\left(f_{i}^{\rm eq}-f_{i}\right)+\left(f^{*}_{i}-f_{i}^{\rm eq}\right),

(831)

where we have only retained terms up to order two. Then introducing characteristic flow size
\mathcal{L}
and velocity
\mathcal{U}
the equation is made non-dimensional as:

\left(\frac{\delta r}{\mathcal{L}}\right)\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\prime}f_{i}+\frac{1}{2}{\left(\frac{\delta r}{\mathcal{L}}\right)}^{2}{\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\prime}}^{2}f_{i}=\frac{\delta t}{\bar{\tau}}\left(f_{i}^{\rm eq}-f_{i}\right)+\left(f^{*}_{i}(\bm{u}^{\prime}+\frac{\delta u}{\mathcal{U}}\delta\bm{u}^{\prime})-f_{i}^{\rm eq}(\bm{u}^{\prime})\right),

(832)

where primed variables denote non-dimensional form and

\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\prime}=\frac{\mathcal{U}}{c}\left(\partial_{t}^{\prime}+\bm{c}_{i}^{\prime}\cdot\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\right),

(833)

where
c=\delta r/\delta t
. Assuming acoustic, i.e.
\frac{\mathcal{U}}{c}\sim 1
and hydrodynamic, i.e.
\frac{\delta r}{\mathcal{L}}\sim\frac{\delta u}{\mathcal{U}}\sim\varepsilon
, scaling and dropping the primes for the sake of readability:

\varepsilon\mathcal{D}_{t}f_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}{\mathcal{D}_{t}}^{2}f_{i}+{O}(\varepsilon^{3})=\frac{\delta t}{\bar{\tau}}\left(f_{i}^{\rm eq}-f_{i}\right)+\left(f^{*}_{i}(\bm{u}+\varepsilon\delta\bm{u})-f_{i}^{\rm eq}(\bm{u})\right).

(834)

Then introducing multi-scale expansions:

\displaystyle f_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{i}^{(0)}+\varepsilon f_{i}^{(1)}+\varepsilon^{2}f_{i}^{(2)}+O(\varepsilon^{3}),

(835)

\displaystyle{f^{*}_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{f^{*}_{i}}^{(0)}+\varepsilon{f^{*}_{i}}^{(1)}+\varepsilon^{2}{f^{*}_{i}}^{(2)}+O(\varepsilon^{3}),

(836)

the following equations are recovered at scales
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon^{2}
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle:\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(1)}f_{i}^{(0)}=-\frac{\delta t}{\bar{\tau}}f_{i}^{(1)}+{f^{*}}_{i}^{(1)},

(837a)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle:\partial_{t}^{(2)}f_{i}^{(0)}+\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(1)}\left(1-\frac{\delta t}{2\bar{\tau}}\right)f_{i}^{(1)}=-\frac{\delta t}{\bar{\tau}}f_{i}^{(2)}+{f^{*}}_{i}^{(2)}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(1)}{f^{*}}_{i}^{(1)},

(837b)

with
f_{i}^{(0)}={f_{i}^{*}}^{(0)}=f_{i}^{\rm eq}
. The moments of the non-local contributions (including both non-ideal contributions to the thermodynamic pressure, surface tension and the correction for the diagonals of the third-order moments tensor) are:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}{f^{*}}_{i}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=0,\forall k>0,

(838a)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\bm{c}_{i}{f^{*}_{i}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\bm{F},

(838b)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\bm{c}_{i}\otimes\bm{c}_{i}{f^{*}_{i}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=(\bm{u}\otimes\bm{F}+{\bm{F}\otimes\bm{u}})+\Phi

(838c)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\bm{c}_{i}\otimes\bm{c}_{i}{f^{*}_{i}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho}\bm{F}\otimes\bm{F}.

(838d)

Taking the moments of the Chapman-Enskog-expanded equation at order
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(839)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho\bm{u}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot P_{0}\bm{I}+\bm{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(840)

while at order
\varepsilon^{2}
the continuity equation is:

\partial_{t}^{(2)}\rho+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\frac{\bm{F}}{2}=0.

(841)

Summing up Eqs.
839
and
841
we recover the continuity equation as:

\partial_{t}\rho+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{U}=0,

(842)

where
\bm{U}=\bm{u}+\frac{\delta t}{2\rho}\bm{F}
. For the momentum equations we have:

\partial_{t}^{(2)}\rho\bm{u}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}^{(1)}\bm{F}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\bm{u}\otimes\bm{F}+{\bm{F}\otimes\bm{u}}\right)+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}\right)\left[\partial_{t}^{(1)}\Pi_{2}^{(0)}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\Pi_{3}^{(0)}\right]\\
-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}\right)\left(\bm{u}\otimes\bm{F}+{\bm{F}\otimes\bm{u}}\right)+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}\Phi=0,

(843)

where
\Pi_{2}^{(0)}
and
\Pi_{3}^{(0)}
are the second- and third-order moments of
f_{i}^{(0)}
defined as:

\displaystyle\Pi_{2}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}+P_{0}\bm{I},

(844)

\displaystyle\Pi_{3}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi_{3}^{\rm MB}-\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\circ\bm{J}-3(P_{0}-\rho\varsigma^{2})\bm{J}

(845)

where
\Pi_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rm MB}=\rho u_{\alpha}u_{\beta}u_{\gamma}+P_{0}{\rm perm}(u_{\alpha}\delta_{\beta\gamma})
is the third-order moment of the Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution, and for the sake of simplicity we have introduced the diagonal rank three tensor
\bm{J}
, with
J_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\delta_{\beta\gamma}
and
\circ
is the Hadamard product.
The contributions in the fourth term on the left hand side can be expanded as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{(1)}\Pi_{2}^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}+\partial_{t}^{(1)}P_{0}\bm{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{u}\otimes\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho\bm{u}+{(\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho\bm{u})\otimes\bm{u}}-\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\partial_{t}^{(1)}\rho+\partial_{t}^{(1)}P_{0}\bm{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}-\left[\bm{u}\otimes\left(\bm{\nabla}P_{0}-\bm{F}\right)+{\left(\bm{\nabla}P_{0}-\bm{F}\right)\otimes\bm{u}}\right]+\partial_{t}^{(1)}P_{0}\bm{I}

(846)

and:

\bm{\nabla}\cdot\Pi_{3}^{(0)}=\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}+\left(\bm{\nabla}P_{0}\bm{u}+{\bm{\nabla}P_{0}\bm{u}}^{\dagger}\right)+(\bm{\nabla}\cdot P_{0}\bm{u})\bm{I}-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left[\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\circ\bm{J}+3(P_{0}-\rho\varsigma^{2})\bm{J}\right],

(847)

resulting in:

\partial_{t}^{(1)}\Pi_{2}^{(0)}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\Pi_{3}^{(0)}=P_{0}\left(\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}+{\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}}^{\dagger}\right)+\left(\bm{u}\otimes\bm{F}+{\bm{u}\otimes\bm{F}}^{\dagger}\right)\\
+\left(\bm{\nabla}\cdot P_{0}\bm{u}+\partial_{t}^{(1)}P_{0}\right)\bm{I}-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left[\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\circ\bm{J}+3(P_{0}-\rho\varsigma^{2})\bm{J}\right].

(848)

Plugging this last equation back into Eq. (
843
):

\partial_{t}^{(2)}\rho\bm{u}+\partial_{t}^{(1)}\frac{\bm{F}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\nabla}\cdot(\bm{u}\otimes\bm{F}+{\bm{F}\otimes\bm{u}})+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}\right)P_{0}\left(\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}+{\bm{\nabla}\bm{u}}^{\dagger}\right)\\
+\bm{\nabla}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}\right)\left(\partial_{t}^{(1)}P_{0}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot P_{0}\bm{u}\right)\\
+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left[\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}\right)\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\circ\bm{J}+3(P_{0}-\rho\varsigma^{2})\bm{J}\right)+\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}\Phi\right]=0.

(849)

where the last term cancels out by setting:

\Phi=\left(1-\frac{\delta t}{2\bar{\tau}}\right)\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}\circ\bm{J}+3(P_{0}-\rho\varsigma^{2})\bm{J}\right),

(850)

and the fourth and fifth terms reduce to the viscous stress tensor by defining
\mu/P_{0}=\left(\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}-\frac{1}{2}\right)
and:

P_{0}\left(\frac{2+D}{D}-\frac{\partial\ln P_{0}}{\partial\ln\rho}\right)\left(\frac{\bar{\tau}}{\delta t}-\frac{1}{2}\right)=\eta.

(851)

Furthermore, using
\bm{U}=\bm{u}+\frac{\delta t}{2\rho}\bm{F}
and:

\rho\bm{U}\otimes\bm{U}=\rho\bm{u}\otimes\bm{u}+\frac{\delta t}{2}(\bm{u}\otimes\bm{F}+{\bm{F}\otimes\bm{u}})+\frac{\delta t^{2}\bm{F}\otimes\bm{F}}{4\rho},

(852)

in combination with the Euler-level equation, and keeping in mind that errors of the form
\bm{\nabla}\cdot\frac{\delta t^{2}\bm{F}\otimes\bm{F}}{4\rho}
in the convective term and
\delta t\bm{\nabla}\mu\left(\bm{\nabla}\frac{\bm{F}}{\rho}+{\bm{\nabla}\frac{\bm{F}}{\rho}}^{\dagger}\right)
in the viscous stress are of order
\varepsilon^{3}
one recovers:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho\bm{U}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\rho\bm{U}\otimes\bm{U}-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\mu\left(\bm{\nabla}\bm{U}+{\bm{\nabla}\bm{U}}^{\dagger}-\frac{2}{D}\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{U}\bm{I}\right)-\bm{\nabla}\cdot\left(\eta\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{U}\right)+{O}(\varepsilon^{3})=0.

(853)
